My family has also been checking on me more because they got worried about how far I’d slipped from my usual routines.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 weeks\n  manifestation: The wired, out-of-control stretch started right after the jewelry-show award and went on for about two weeks.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 month\n  manifestation: The depressed, guilty feeling has been hanging over me for about a month now.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S039\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you ever make big decisions without thinking them through, like spending a lot of money or quitting a project?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I’ll jump between tasks, yeah, but big decisions… I can’t really think of a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or feel restless at home or when you're not working, too?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not only at work, but work is where I notice it most because there’s proof I messed up, you know? Like the order sheet is sitting right there and somehow I’ve gone from reading a customer email to noticing a saw mark, then hearing something outside, and then I look back and I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nAt home it’s kind of similar, I’ll start one thing and then—well, like, I’ll remember I left a tray out at the shop, or think about a design, and then I’m not really watching whatever’s on. But the big thing that made me come in is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or feel restless even on weekends or during vacations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… I mean, weekends are harder to judge because I don’t have the work order sitting there proving I lost track. But it’s the same kind of thing, my mind just won’t stay put.\n\nAt the bench it’s really obvious—like I’ll be answering a customer email, then notice a saw mark, then there’s some noise outside, and suddenly I’m looking back like, wait, what was I even supposed to be doing with this order? I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t put an age on it like, “oh, I was 14” or something. It’s more like I only started really noticing it when it began messing with my work.

I don’t really remember school being like that, no — or I don’t remember anyone making a big issue of it. I wasn’t, like, getting sent out of class for running around or anything, at least not that I can recall.

I did like shop class, though — anything where I could actually use my hands. Sitting through long lectures was boring, but I figured that was normal. Most kids don’t love sitting there while somebody talks about, I don’t know, history dates or whatever. I remember doodling sometimes, and I used to draw little ring designs in the margins, which is funny now because that’s basically what I ended up doing for work, just with actual metal and stones.

So… I guess I don’t know. Nothing really stands out from childhood as a problem with sitting still or paying attention.

My parents never made a big thing of it, at least not that I remember. So… I can’t really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any difficulties with paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember school being like that, no — or I don’t remember anyone making a big issue of it. Elementary school… I don’t really have a clear memory of that being a thing. Like I said, nobody was pulling my parents in for meetings about me bouncing off the walls or not listening, at least not that I know of.

Then I look back and realize I’ve lost the whole work order.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ve been doing jewelry work a while, and usually I can keep track of tiny details—stones, measurements, all that—but lately my attention just keeps jumping around.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with your attention causing you a lot of stress or worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is stressing me out, because that’s kind of the whole reason I came in. I’ll be at the bench and I’m supposed to be reading a customer email, then I notice some saw mark on a piece, then there’s a noise outside or something, and before I know it I’ve totally lost the work order.\n\nAnd then I start worrying I’m going to miss something important. In jewelry, tiny details matter, you know—like one wrong measurement and it’s not just a little mistake. So yeah, it’s been bothering me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Sitting at the bench has been hard lately. I’ll start bouncing my leg or shifting around, and then instead of staying with one thing, I’m up and messing with something else—like I’ll sketch a new watch case, then decide I need to email a supplier, then somehow I’m rearranging the display trays.\n\nAnd the weird part is I’ll keep doing that even after closing, when I should just be done and go home. I don’t know, it feels like my body’s still trying to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Um… impulsive how? I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it that. Sometimes I’ll just do something before I’ve really lined it up in my head, like grab a tool or start on another little task because it’s right there, but I don’t know if that’s “acting on impulse” exactly.\n\nIt’s like… in the shop, there’s always something catching your eye. A loose clasp, a stone tray left out, a note from a customer, and then I’m already halfway into that. But I’m not sure if that’s what you mean or if you mean, like, big reckless decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make big decisions without thinking them through, like spending a lot of money or quitting a project?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, spending money at the shop can look big, because tools and stones are expensive, so it’s hard to tell what counts as “a lot,” you know? Like even a small parcel can be—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nI don’t think I’m, like, suddenly quitting projects or making some huge life decision out of nowhere. I’ll jump between tasks, yeah, but big decisions… I can’t really think of a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or feel restless at home or when you're not working, too?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not only at work, but work is where I notice it most because there’s proof I messed up, you know? Like the order sheet is sitting right there and somehow I’ve gone from reading a customer email to noticing a saw mark, then hearing something outside, and then I look back and I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nAt home it’s kind of similar, I’ll start one thing and then—well, like, I’ll remember I left a tray out at the shop, or think about a design, and then I’m not really watching whatever’s on. But the big thing that made me come in is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or feel restless even on weekends or during vacations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… I mean, weekends are harder to judge because I don’t have the work order sitting there proving I lost track. But it’s the same kind of thing, my mind just won’t stay put.\n\nAt the bench it’s really obvious—like I’ll be answering a customer email, then notice a saw mark, then there’s some noise outside, and suddenly I’m looking back like, wait, what was I even supposed to be doing with this order? I just keep moving from one thing to another, like sitting still feels wrong somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with your attention and restlessness, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I’ll be at the bench, start with a customer email, then I see a saw mark, then I hear something outside, and when I look back I’ve completely lost where I was with the work order. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not that I remember, no. I don’t have this memory of my parents getting called in or teachers saying, “He can’t focus” or “He won’t sit still.”

My mom did used to complain I’d leave little scraps and bits of wire around if I was tinkering with stuff, but that was more like making a mess than, you know, school concerns. I had this little box of watch parts once—gears and screws and things—and I’d get really into sorting them, so if anything I could focus on that for a long time. But school stuff… I don’t know. Nobody made it sound like a problem when I was growing up, at least as far as I remember.

Then I look back and realize I’ve lost the whole work order.\n\nIt’s embarrassing because I’ve been doing jewelry work a while, and usually I can keep track of tiny details—stones, measurements, all that—but lately my attention just keeps jumping around.\n\nDoctor: Are these difficulties with your attention causing you a lot of stress or worry?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it is stressing me out, because that’s kind of the whole reason I came in. I’ll be at the bench and I’m supposed to be reading a customer email, then I notice some saw mark on a piece, then there’s a noise outside or something, and before I know it I’ve totally lost the work order.\n\nAnd then I start worrying I’m going to miss something important. In jewelry, tiny details matter, you know—like one wrong measurement and it’s not just a little mistake. So yeah, it’s been bothering me a lot.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Sitting at the bench has been hard lately. I’ll start bouncing my leg or shifting around, and then instead of staying with one thing, I’m up and messing with something else—like I’ll sketch a new watch case, then decide I need to email a supplier, then somehow I’m rearranging the display trays.\n\nAnd the weird part is I’ll keep doing that even after closing, when I should just be done and go home. I don’t know, it feels like my body’s still trying to keep moving.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: Um… impulsive how? I mean, I don’t know if I’d call it that. Sometimes I’ll just do something before I’ve really lined it up in my head, like grab a tool or start on another little task because it’s right there, but I don’t know if that’s “acting on impulse” exactly.\n\nIt’s like… in the shop, there’s always something catching your eye. A loose clasp, a stone tray left out, a note from a customer, and then I’m already halfway into that. But I’m not sure if that’s what you mean or if you mean, like, big reckless decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make big decisions without thinking them through, like spending a lot of money or quitting a project?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, spending money at the shop can look big, because tools and stones are expensive, so it’s hard to tell what counts as “a lot,” you know? Like even a small parcel can be—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nI don’t think I’m, like, suddenly quitting projects or making some huge life decision out of nowhere. I’ll jump between tasks, yeah, but big decisions… I can’t really think of a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or feel restless at home or when you're not working, too?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not only at work, but work is where I notice it most because there’s proof I messed up, you know? Like the order sheet is sitting right there and somehow I’ve gone from reading a customer email to noticing a saw mark, then hearing something outside, and then I look back and I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nAt home it’s kind of similar, I’ll start one thing and then—well, like, I’ll remember I left a tray out at the shop, or think about a design, and then I’m not really watching whatever’s on. But the big thing that made me come in is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or feel restless even on weekends or during vacations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… I mean, weekends are harder to judge because I don’t have the work order sitting there proving I lost track. But it’s the same kind of thing, my mind just won’t stay put.\n\nAt the bench it’s really obvious—like I’ll be answering a customer email, then notice a saw mark, then there’s some noise outside, and suddenly I’m looking back like, wait, what was I even supposed to be doing with this order? That’s honestly what brought me in, more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're relaxing or doing leisure activities at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I’m supposed to be relaxing, I feel like I can’t just sit there. At the bench it’s worse — I’ll be sketching a new watch case, then suddenly I’m emailing suppliers, then I’m rearranging display trays, and next thing I know it’s way after closing.\n\nIt’s not even like I planned to stay late. I just keep moving from one thing to another, like sitting still feels wrong somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with your attention and restlessness, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve probably always been the kind of guy who notices details — which helps with jewelry, honestly, because a tiny saw mark or a crooked setting can drive me nuts — but this feels more recent.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. I’ll be at the bench, start with a customer email, then I see a saw mark, then I hear something outside, and when I look back I’ve completely lost where I was with the work order. It’s like I keep getting pulled off track before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with your attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t put an age on it like, “oh, I was 14” or something. It’s more like I only started really noticing it when it began messing with my work.\n\nI mean, thinking back, I probably had days where I was scattered, but everybody has that, right? And with jewelry work, you get used to jumping between tiny details anyway — stones, settings, orders, customers coming in — so it’s hard to tell what was just the job and what was me. So… I don’t know exactly how old I was when it started.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you had trouble paying attention or behaved restlessly when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really remember being told that as a kid, like teachers saying I couldn’t sit still or anything.\n\nI mean, I was probably busy with my hands a lot — I liked taking little things apart, messing with bits of metal or watches, stuff like that — but I don’t know if that was “restless” or just being a kid. My parents never made a big thing of it, at least not that I remember. I just keep moving from one thing to another, like sitting still feels wrong somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with your attention and restlessness, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve probably always been the kind of guy who notices details — which helps with jewelry, honestly, because a tiny saw mark or a crooked setting can drive me nuts — but this feels more recent.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. I’ll be at the bench, start with a customer email, then I see a saw mark, then I hear something outside, and when I look back I’ve completely lost where I was with the work order. It’s like I keep getting pulled off track before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with your attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t put an age on it like, “oh, I was 14” or something. It’s more like I only started really noticing it when it began messing with my work.\n\nI mean, thinking back, I probably had days where I was scattered, but everybody has that, right? And with jewelry work, you get used to jumping between tiny details anyway — stones, settings, orders, customers coming in — so it’s hard to tell what was just the job and what was me. So… I don’t know exactly how old I was when it started.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you had trouble paying attention or behaved restlessly when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really remember being told that as a kid, like teachers saying I couldn’t sit still or anything.\n\nI mean, I was probably busy with my hands a lot — I liked taking little things apart, messing with bits of metal or watches, stuff like that — but I don’t know if that was “restless” or just being a kid. My parents never made a big thing of it, at least not that I remember. So… I can’t really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any difficulties with paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember school being like that, no — or I don’t remember anyone making a big issue of it. I wasn’t, like, getting sent out of class for running around or anything, at least not that I can recall.\n\nI did like shop class, though — anything where I could actually use my hands. Sitting through long lectures was boring, but I figured that was normal. Most kids don’t love sitting there while somebody talks about, I don’t know, history dates or whatever. I remember doodling sometimes, and I used to draw little ring designs in the margins, which is funny now because that’s basically what I ended up doing for work, just with actual metal and stones.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. Nothing really stands out from childhood as a problem with sitting still or paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or sitting still when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Elementary school… I don’t really have a clear memory of that being a thing. Like I said, nobody was pulling my parents in for meetings about me bouncing off the walls or not listening, at least not that I know of.\n\nI remember more random stuff, honestly — like the smell of those old pencil sharpeners, and I had this one teacher who kept a jar of little colored stones on her desk, and I used to stare at those because I thought they were cool. I’ll jump between tasks, yeah, but big decisions… I can’t really think of a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or feel restless at home or when you're not working, too?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not only at work, but work is where I notice it most because there’s proof I messed up, you know? Like the order sheet is sitting right there and somehow I’ve gone from reading a customer email to noticing a saw mark, then hearing something outside, and then I look back and I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nAt home it’s kind of similar, I’ll start one thing and then—well, like, I’ll remember I left a tray out at the shop, or think about a design, and then I’m not really watching whatever’s on. But the big thing that made me come in is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or feel restless even on weekends or during vacations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… I mean, weekends are harder to judge because I don’t have the work order sitting there proving I lost track. But it’s the same kind of thing, my mind just won’t stay put.\n\nAt the bench it’s really obvious—like I’ll be answering a customer email, then notice a saw mark, then there’s some noise outside, and suddenly I’m looking back like, wait, what was I even supposed to be doing with this order? That’s honestly what brought me in, more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're relaxing or doing leisure activities at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I’m supposed to be relaxing, I feel like I can’t just sit there. At the bench it’s worse — I’ll be sketching a new watch case, then suddenly I’m emailing suppliers, then I’m rearranging display trays, and next thing I know it’s way after closing.\n\nIt’s not even like I planned to stay late. I just keep moving from one thing to another, like sitting still feels wrong somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with your attention and restlessness, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve probably always been the kind of guy who notices details — which helps with jewelry, honestly, because a tiny saw mark or a crooked setting can drive me nuts — but this feels more recent.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. I’ll be at the bench, start with a customer email, then I see a saw mark, then I hear something outside, and when I look back I’ve completely lost where I was with the work order. It’s like I keep getting pulled off track before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with your attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t put an age on it like, “oh, I was 14” or something. It’s more like I only started really noticing it when it began messing with my work.\n\nI mean, thinking back, I probably had days where I was scattered, but everybody has that, right? And with jewelry work, you get used to jumping between tiny details anyway — stones, settings, orders, customers coming in — so it’s hard to tell what was just the job and what was me. So… I don’t know exactly how old I was when it started.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you had trouble paying attention or behaved restlessly when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really remember being told that as a kid, like teachers saying I couldn’t sit still or anything.\n\nI mean, I was probably busy with my hands a lot — I liked taking little things apart, messing with bits of metal or watches, stuff like that — but I don’t know if that was “restless” or just being a kid. My parents never made a big thing of it, at least not that I remember. So… I can’t really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any difficulties with paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember school being like that, no — or I don’t remember anyone making a big issue of it. I wasn’t, like, getting sent out of class for running around or anything, at least not that I can recall.\n\nI did like shop class, though — anything where I could actually use my hands. Sitting through long lectures was boring, but I figured that was normal. Most kids don’t love sitting there while somebody talks about, I don’t know, history dates or whatever. I remember doodling sometimes, and I used to draw little ring designs in the margins, which is funny now because that’s basically what I ended up doing for work, just with actual metal and stones.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. Nothing really stands out from childhood as a problem with sitting still or paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or sitting still when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Elementary school… I don’t really have a clear memory of that being a thing. Like I said, nobody was pulling my parents in for meetings about me bouncing off the walls or not listening, at least not that I know of.\n\nI remember more random stuff, honestly — like the smell of those old pencil sharpeners, and I had this one teacher who kept a jar of little colored stones on her desk, and I used to stare at those because I thought they were cool. Which, yeah, maybe that sounds like not paying attention, but I don’t know. I was a kid.\n\nSo… I can’t really say I had problems with it back then. Nothing specific comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever express concern about your ability to focus or sit still when you were growing up?\n\nPatient: Not that I remember, no. I don’t have this memory of my parents getting called in or teachers saying, “He can’t focus” or “He won’t sit still.”\n\nMy mom did used to complain I’d leave little scraps and bits of wire around if I was tinkering with stuff, but that was more like making a mess than, you know, school concerns. I had this little box of watch parts once—gears and screws and things—and I’d get really into sorting them, so if anything I could focus on that for a long time. But school stuff… I don’t know. Nobody made it sound like a problem when I was growing up, at least as far as I remember.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any symptoms like difficulty paying attention or restlessness before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Before 12? I mean… I don’t think so, not in any way that anyone pointed out. I’m kind of going off old memories, and those are fuzzy.\n\nI remember being bored in class sometimes, but I also remember getting really absorbed in little things, like drawing shapes or messing with small parts. My dad had this toolbox with tiny screws and washers, and I’d sort them out for no real reason. So it’s hard for me to say that was a “symptom,” you know?\n\nBut no, I don’t remember having some clear problem with paying attention or being restless before that age.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or sitting still that you remember from when you were little, before you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I know you’re asking it a little differently, but… I still don’t really remember that being a problem before I was a teenager.\n\nLike, I can remember little kid stuff — sitting at the kitchen table with beads or bits of metal, trying to line them up by color or size, and my mom telling me not to lose them in the carpet because then someone would step on them. Funny what sticks in your head. But I don’t remember being the kid who couldn’t stay in his seat or was always getting in trouble for not paying attention.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure, but nothing clear like that comes to mind.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Nobody made it sound like a problem when I was growing up, at least as far as I remember.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any symptoms like difficulty paying attention or restlessness before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Before 12? I mean… I don’t think so, not in any way that anyone pointed out. I’m kind of going off old memories, and those are fuzzy.\n\nI remember being bored in class sometimes, but I also remember getting really absorbed in little things, like drawing shapes or messing with small parts. My dad had this toolbox with tiny screws and washers, and I’d sort them out for no real reason. So it’s hard for me to say that was a “symptom,” you know?\n\nBut no, I don’t remember having some clear problem with paying attention or being restless before that age.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or sitting still that you remember from when you were little, before you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I know you’re asking it a little differently, but… I still don’t really remember that being a problem before I was a teenager.\n\nLike, I can remember little kid stuff — sitting at the kitchen table with beads or bits of metal, trying to line them up by color or size, and my mom telling me not to lose them in the carpet because then someone would step on them. Funny what sticks in your head. But I don’t remember being the kid who couldn’t stay in his seat or was always getting in trouble for not paying attention.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure, but nothing clear like that comes to mind.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid you have any notable difficulties with paying attention or sitting still that were pointed out by your parents, teachers, or other adults when you were a child?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / jeweler_or_precious_stone_or_metal_worker / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really, no. I mean, nobody ever sat me down and said that was a problem. I don’t remember teachers calling home about me not sitting still or anything like that.

I was kind of a kid who’d get into little projects—like taking apart cheap watches or sorting tiny pieces, which is funny now because of the work I ended up doing—but that could look like focus, not not focusing. My mom would get annoyed because I’d leave stuff everywhere, little screws and bits on the floor, and she’d say someone was going to step on them. But that was more about the mess.

So… no, I don’t think any adults pointed out attention or sitting still as a notable issue when I was a child.

I just keep moving from one thing to another, like sitting still feels wrong somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with your attention and restlessness, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve probably always been the kind of guy who notices details — which helps with jewelry, honestly, because a tiny saw mark or a crooked setting can drive me nuts — but this feels more recent.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. I’ll be at the bench, start with a customer email, then I see a saw mark, then I hear something outside, and when I look back I’ve completely lost where I was with the work order. It’s like I keep getting pulled off track before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with your attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t put an age on it like, “oh, I was 14” or something. I don’t really remember being told that as a kid, like teachers saying I couldn’t sit still or anything.\n\nI mean, I was probably busy with my hands a lot — I liked taking little things apart, messing with bits of metal or watches, stuff like that — but I don’t know if that was “restless” or just being a kid. But the big thing that made me come in is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or feel restless even on weekends or during vacations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… I mean, weekends are harder to judge because I don’t have the work order sitting there proving I lost track. But it’s the same kind of thing, my mind just won’t stay put.\n\nAt the bench it’s really obvious—like I’ll be answering a customer email, then notice a saw mark, then there’s some noise outside, and suddenly I’m looking back like, wait, what was I even supposed to be doing with this order? Even when I’m supposed to be relaxing, I feel like I can’t just sit there. At the bench it’s worse — I’ll be sketching a new watch case, then suddenly I’m emailing suppliers, then I’m rearranging display trays, and next thing I know it’s way after closing.\n\nIt’s not even like I planned to stay late. I just keep moving from one thing to another, like sitting still feels wrong somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with your attention and restlessness, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve probably always been the kind of guy who notices details — which helps with jewelry, honestly, because a tiny saw mark or a crooked setting can drive me nuts — but this feels more recent.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. I’ll be at the bench, start with a customer email, then I see a saw mark, then I hear something outside, and when I look back I’ve completely lost where I was with the work order. It’s like I keep getting pulled off track before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with your attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t put an age on it like, “oh, I was 14” or something. It’s more like I only started really noticing it when it began messing with my work.\n\nI mean, thinking back, I probably had days where I was scattered, but everybody has that, right? And with jewelry work, you get used to jumping between tiny details anyway — stones, settings, orders, customers coming in — so it’s hard to tell what was just the job and what was me. So… I don’t know exactly how old I was when it started.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you had trouble paying attention or behaved restlessly when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really remember being told that as a kid, like teachers saying I couldn’t sit still or anything.\n\nI mean, I was probably busy with my hands a lot — I liked taking little things apart, messing with bits of metal or watches, stuff like that — but I don’t know if that was “restless” or just being a kid. My parents never made a big thing of it, at least not that I remember. So… I can’t really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any difficulties with paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember school being like that, no — or I don’t remember anyone making a big issue of it. I wasn’t, like, getting sent out of class for running around or anything, at least not that I can recall.\n\nI did like shop class, though — anything where I could actually use my hands. Sometimes I’ll just do something before I’ve really lined it up in my head, like grab a tool or start on another little task because it’s right there, but I don’t know if that’s “acting on impulse” exactly.\n\nIt’s like… in the shop, there’s always something catching your eye. A loose clasp, a stone tray left out, a note from a customer, and then I’m already halfway into that. But I’m not sure if that’s what you mean or if you mean, like, big reckless decisions.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make big decisions without thinking them through, like spending a lot of money or quitting a project?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, spending money at the shop can look big, because tools and stones are expensive, so it’s hard to tell what counts as “a lot,” you know? Like even a small parcel can be—sorry, that’s probably not what you’re asking.\n\nI don’t think I’m, like, suddenly quitting projects or making some huge life decision out of nowhere. I’ll jump between tasks, yeah, but big decisions… I can’t really think of a clear example.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus or feel restless at home or when you're not working, too?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, it’s not only at work, but work is where I notice it most because there’s proof I messed up, you know? Like the order sheet is sitting right there and somehow I’ve gone from reading a customer email to noticing a saw mark, then hearing something outside, and then I look back and I’ve lost the whole thread of what I was supposed to be doing.\n\nAt home it’s kind of similar, I’ll start one thing and then—well, like, I’ll remember I left a tray out at the shop, or think about a design, and then I’m not really watching whatever’s on. But the big thing that made me come in is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or feel restless even on weekends or during vacations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… I mean, weekends are harder to judge because I don’t have the work order sitting there proving I lost track. But it’s the same kind of thing, my mind just won’t stay put.\n\nAt the bench it’s really obvious—like I’ll be answering a customer email, then notice a saw mark, then there’s some noise outside, and suddenly I’m looking back like, wait, what was I even supposed to be doing with this order? That’s honestly what brought me in, more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're relaxing or doing leisure activities at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I’m supposed to be relaxing, I feel like I can’t just sit there. At the bench it’s worse — I’ll be sketching a new watch case, then suddenly I’m emailing suppliers, then I’m rearranging display trays, and next thing I know it’s way after closing.\n\nIt’s not even like I planned to stay late. I just keep moving from one thing to another, like sitting still feels wrong somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with your attention and restlessness, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve probably always been the kind of guy who notices details — which helps with jewelry, honestly, because a tiny saw mark or a crooked setting can drive me nuts — but this feels more recent.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. I’ll be at the bench, start with a customer email, then I see a saw mark, then I hear something outside, and when I look back I’ve completely lost where I was with the work order. It’s like I keep getting pulled off track before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with your attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t put an age on it like, “oh, I was 14” or something. It’s more like I only started really noticing it when it began messing with my work.\n\nI mean, thinking back, I probably had days where I was scattered, but everybody has that, right? And with jewelry work, you get used to jumping between tiny details anyway — stones, settings, orders, customers coming in — so it’s hard to tell what was just the job and what was me. So… I don’t know exactly how old I was when it started.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you had trouble paying attention or behaved restlessly when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really remember being told that as a kid, like teachers saying I couldn’t sit still or anything.\n\nI mean, I was probably busy with my hands a lot — I liked taking little things apart, messing with bits of metal or watches, stuff like that — but I don’t know if that was “restless” or just being a kid. My parents never made a big thing of it, at least not that I remember. So… I can’t really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any difficulties with paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember school being like that, no — or I don’t remember anyone making a big issue of it. I wasn’t, like, getting sent out of class for running around or anything, at least not that I can recall.\n\nI did like shop class, though — anything where I could actually use my hands. Sitting through long lectures was boring, but I figured that was normal. Most kids don’t love sitting there while somebody talks about, I don’t know, history dates or whatever. I remember doodling sometimes, and I used to draw little ring designs in the margins, which is funny now because that’s basically what I ended up doing for work, just with actual metal and stones.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. Nothing really stands out from childhood as a problem with sitting still or paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or sitting still when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Elementary school… I don’t really have a clear memory of that being a thing. Like I said, nobody was pulling my parents in for meetings about me bouncing off the walls or not listening, at least not that I know of.\n\nI remember more random stuff, honestly — like the smell of those old pencil sharpeners, and I had this one teacher who kept a jar of little colored stones on her desk, and I used to stare at those because I thought they were cool. Which, yeah, maybe that sounds like not paying attention, but I don’t know. I was a kid.\n\nSo… I can’t really say I had problems with it back then. Nothing specific comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever express concern about your ability to focus or sit still when you were growing up?\n\nPatient: Not that I remember, no. I don’t have this memory of my parents getting called in or teachers saying, “He can’t focus” or “He won’t sit still.”\n\nMy mom did used to complain I’d leave little scraps and bits of wire around if I was tinkering with stuff, but that was more like making a mess than, you know, school concerns. But the big thing that made me come in is my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you have trouble focusing or feel restless even on weekends or during vacations?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so… I mean, weekends are harder to judge because I don’t have the work order sitting there proving I lost track. That’s honestly what brought me in, more than anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're relaxing or doing leisure activities at home?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. Even when I’m supposed to be relaxing, I feel like I can’t just sit there. At the bench it’s worse — I’ll be sketching a new watch case, then suddenly I’m emailing suppliers, then I’m rearranging display trays, and next thing I know it’s way after closing.\n\nIt’s not even like I planned to stay late. I just keep moving from one thing to another, like sitting still feels wrong somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with your attention and restlessness, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve probably always been the kind of guy who notices details — which helps with jewelry, honestly, because a tiny saw mark or a crooked setting can drive me nuts — but this feels more recent.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. I’ll be at the bench, start with a customer email, then I see a saw mark, then I hear something outside, and when I look back I’ve completely lost where I was with the work order. It’s like I keep getting pulled off track before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with your attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t put an age on it like, “oh, I was 14” or something. It’s more like I only started really noticing it when it began messing with my work.\n\nI mean, thinking back, I probably had days where I was scattered, but everybody has that, right? And with jewelry work, you get used to jumping between tiny details anyway — stones, settings, orders, customers coming in — so it’s hard to tell what was just the job and what was me. So… I don’t know exactly how old I was when it started.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you had trouble paying attention or behaved restlessly when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really remember being told that as a kid, like teachers saying I couldn’t sit still or anything.\n\nI mean, I was probably busy with my hands a lot — I liked taking little things apart, messing with bits of metal or watches, stuff like that — but I don’t know if that was “restless” or just being a kid. My parents never made a big thing of it, at least not that I remember. So… I can’t really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any difficulties with paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember school being like that, no — or I don’t remember anyone making a big issue of it. I wasn’t, like, getting sent out of class for running around or anything, at least not that I can recall.\n\nI did like shop class, though — anything where I could actually use my hands. Sitting through long lectures was boring, but I figured that was normal. Most kids don’t love sitting there while somebody talks about, I don’t know, history dates or whatever. I remember doodling sometimes, and I used to draw little ring designs in the margins, which is funny now because that’s basically what I ended up doing for work, just with actual metal and stones.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. Nothing really stands out from childhood as a problem with sitting still or paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or sitting still when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Elementary school… I don’t really have a clear memory of that being a thing. Like I said, nobody was pulling my parents in for meetings about me bouncing off the walls or not listening, at least not that I know of.\n\nI remember more random stuff, honestly — like the smell of those old pencil sharpeners, and I had this one teacher who kept a jar of little colored stones on her desk, and I used to stare at those because I thought they were cool. Which, yeah, maybe that sounds like not paying attention, but I don’t know. I was a kid.\n\nSo… I can’t really say I had problems with it back then. Nothing specific comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever express concern about your ability to focus or sit still when you were growing up?\n\nPatient: Not that I remember, no. I don’t have this memory of my parents getting called in or teachers saying, “He can’t focus” or “He won’t sit still.”\n\nMy mom did used to complain I’d leave little scraps and bits of wire around if I was tinkering with stuff, but that was more like making a mess than, you know, school concerns. I had this little box of watch parts once—gears and screws and things—and I’d get really into sorting them, so if anything I could focus on that for a long time. But school stuff… I don’t know. Nobody made it sound like a problem when I was growing up, at least as far as I remember.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any symptoms like difficulty paying attention or restlessness before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Before 12? I mean… I don’t think so, not in any way that anyone pointed out. I’m kind of going off old memories, and those are fuzzy.\n\nI remember being bored in class sometimes, but I also remember getting really absorbed in little things, like drawing shapes or messing with small parts. My dad had this toolbox with tiny screws and washers, and I’d sort them out for no real reason. So it’s hard for me to say that was a “symptom,” you know?\n\nBut no, I don’t remember having some clear problem with paying attention or being restless before that age.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or sitting still that you remember from when you were little, before you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I know you’re asking it a little differently, but… I still don’t really remember that being a problem before I was a teenager.\n\nLike, I can remember little kid stuff — sitting at the kitchen table with beads or bits of metal, trying to line them up by color or size, and my mom telling me not to lose them in the carpet because then someone would step on them. Funny what sticks in your head. But I don’t remember being the kid who couldn’t stay in his seat or was always getting in trouble for not paying attention.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure, but nothing clear like that comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any notable difficulties with paying attention or sitting still that were pointed out by your parents, teachers, or other adults when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, nobody ever sat me down and said that was a problem. I don’t remember teachers calling home about me not sitting still or anything like that.\n\nI was kind of a kid who’d get into little projects—like taking apart cheap watches or sorting tiny pieces, which is funny now because of the work I ended up doing—but that could look like focus, not not focusing. My mom would get annoyed because I’d leave stuff everywhere, little screws and bits on the floor, and she’d say someone was going to step on them. But that was more about the mess.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think any adults pointed out attention or sitting still as a notable issue when I was a child.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with paying attention and feeling restless have been present in some form since you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so… or at least I can’t say that clearly. When I think of being a kid, I don’t picture myself bouncing off the walls or getting told I couldn’t pay attention.\n\nI mean, I was always doing little detailed stuff, like sorting tiny things or fiddling with parts, and now I work with small metal and stones, so maybe that was just kind of always my thing. But I don’t know if that counts as anything.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I know it’s been there since childhood. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, mostly with family, I guess. Friends too, but my family noticed it more because they know my routines.\n\nLike, I missed this major custom delivery at the shop, which was… really bad. And then my records got so messy and out of order that I had to stop taking new work for now. I keep thinking, you know, it’s just paperwork, but with stones and metal and orders, one wrong note can turn into a huge problem.\n\nMy family started checking on me more after that. Not in a mean way, just… worried. Like they could tell I’d slipped pretty far from how I usually keep things. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Consistently? I don’t think so. Not like this.

What really brought me in is that lately my mind just won’t stay on one thing. Like at the bench, I’ll be looking at a customer email, then I notice a saw mark, then there’s a noise outside, and suddenly I realize I’ve completely lost the thread of the work order.

And with my kind of work, you can’t really do that. I mean, a tiny measurement or one note about a stone—if I lose track of that, it’s not just annoying, it can mess up the whole job. But no, I wouldn’t say it’s been the same all through adulthood. It feels more like something that’s gotten really bad now.

It’s like I keep getting pulled off track before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with your attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t put an age on it like, “oh, I was 14” or something. I just keep moving from one thing to another, like sitting still feels wrong somehow.\n\nDoctor: Have you always had trouble with your attention and restlessness, or is this something that has developed more recently?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s always been like this, no. I mean, I’ve probably always been the kind of guy who notices details — which helps with jewelry, honestly, because a tiny saw mark or a crooked setting can drive me nuts — but this feels more recent.\n\nWhat really made me come in is that my mind just won’t stay on one thing anymore. I’ll be at the bench, start with a customer email, then I see a saw mark, then I hear something outside, and when I look back I’ve completely lost where I was with the work order. It’s like I keep getting pulled off track before I even realize it.\n\nDoctor: How old were you when you first started noticing these problems with your attention and restlessness?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure, honestly. I can’t put an age on it like, “oh, I was 14” or something. It’s more like I only started really noticing it when it began messing with my work.\n\nI mean, thinking back, I probably had days where I was scattered, but everybody has that, right? And with jewelry work, you get used to jumping between tiny details anyway — stones, settings, orders, customers coming in — so it’s hard to tell what was just the job and what was me. So… I don’t know exactly how old I was when it started.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you had trouble paying attention or behaved restlessly when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really remember being told that as a kid, like teachers saying I couldn’t sit still or anything.\n\nI mean, I was probably busy with my hands a lot — I liked taking little things apart, messing with bits of metal or watches, stuff like that — but I don’t know if that was “restless” or just being a kid. My parents never made a big thing of it, at least not that I remember. So… I can’t really say for sure.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any difficulties with paying attention or sitting still in school when you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really remember school being like that, no — or I don’t remember anyone making a big issue of it. I wasn’t, like, getting sent out of class for running around or anything, at least not that I can recall.\n\nI did like shop class, though — anything where I could actually use my hands. Sitting through long lectures was boring, but I figured that was normal. Most kids don’t love sitting there while somebody talks about, I don’t know, history dates or whatever. I remember doodling sometimes, and I used to draw little ring designs in the margins, which is funny now because that’s basically what I ended up doing for work, just with actual metal and stones.\n\nSo… I guess I don’t know. Nothing really stands out from childhood as a problem with sitting still or paying attention.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or sitting still when you were very young, like in elementary school?\n\nPatient: Elementary school… I don’t really have a clear memory of that being a thing. Like I said, nobody was pulling my parents in for meetings about me bouncing off the walls or not listening, at least not that I know of.\n\nI remember more random stuff, honestly — like the smell of those old pencil sharpeners, and I had this one teacher who kept a jar of little colored stones on her desk, and I used to stare at those because I thought they were cool. Which, yeah, maybe that sounds like not paying attention, but I don’t know. I was a kid.\n\nSo… I can’t really say I had problems with it back then. Nothing specific comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers ever express concern about your ability to focus or sit still when you were growing up?\n\nPatient: Not that I remember, no. I don’t have this memory of my parents getting called in or teachers saying, “He can’t focus” or “He won’t sit still.”\n\nMy mom did used to complain I’d leave little scraps and bits of wire around if I was tinkering with stuff, but that was more like making a mess than, you know, school concerns. I had this little box of watch parts once—gears and screws and things—and I’d get really into sorting them, so if anything I could focus on that for a long time. But school stuff… I don’t know. Nobody made it sound like a problem when I was growing up, at least as far as I remember.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience any symptoms like difficulty paying attention or restlessness before the age of 12?\n\nPatient: Before 12? I mean… I don’t think so, not in any way that anyone pointed out. I’m kind of going off old memories, and those are fuzzy.\n\nI remember being bored in class sometimes, but I also remember getting really absorbed in little things, like drawing shapes or messing with small parts. My dad had this toolbox with tiny screws and washers, and I’d sort them out for no real reason. So it’s hard for me to say that was a “symptom,” you know?\n\nBut no, I don’t remember having some clear problem with paying attention or being restless before that age.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any problems with paying attention or sitting still that you remember from when you were little, before you were a teenager?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I know you’re asking it a little differently, but… I still don’t really remember that being a problem before I was a teenager.\n\nLike, I can remember little kid stuff — sitting at the kitchen table with beads or bits of metal, trying to line them up by color or size, and my mom telling me not to lose them in the carpet because then someone would step on them. Funny what sticks in your head. But I don’t remember being the kid who couldn’t stay in his seat or was always getting in trouble for not paying attention.\n\nSo… I guess the honest answer is I’m not sure, but nothing clear like that comes to mind.\n\nDoctor: Did you have any notable difficulties with paying attention or sitting still that were pointed out by your parents, teachers, or other adults when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, nobody ever sat me down and said that was a problem. I don’t remember teachers calling home about me not sitting still or anything like that.\n\nI was kind of a kid who’d get into little projects—like taking apart cheap watches or sorting tiny pieces, which is funny now because of the work I ended up doing—but that could look like focus, not not focusing. My mom would get annoyed because I’d leave stuff everywhere, little screws and bits on the floor, and she’d say someone was going to step on them. But that was more about the mess.\n\nSo… no, I don’t think any adults pointed out attention or sitting still as a notable issue when I was a child.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with paying attention and feeling restless have been present in some form since you were a child?\n\nPatient: I don’t really think so… or at least I can’t say that clearly. When I think of being a kid, I don’t picture myself bouncing off the walls or getting told I couldn’t pay attention.\n\nI mean, I was always doing little detailed stuff, like sorting tiny things or fiddling with parts, and now I work with small metal and stones, so maybe that was just kind of always my thing. But I don’t know if that counts as anything.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I know it’s been there since childhood. I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Have your difficulties with attention and restlessness affected your relationships with friends or family?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, mostly with family, I guess. Friends too, but my family noticed it more because they know my routines.\n\nLike, I missed this major custom delivery at the shop, which was… really bad. And then my records got so messy and out of order that I had to stop taking new work for now. I keep thinking, you know, it’s just paperwork, but with stones and metal and orders, one wrong note can turn into a huge problem.\n\nMy family started checking on me more after that. Not in a mean way, just… worried. Like they could tell I’d slipped pretty far from how I usually keep things. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced significant difficulties with attention and restlessness consistently throughout your adult life?\n\nPatient: Consistently? I don’t think so. Not like this.\n\nWhat really brought me in is that lately my mind just won’t stay on one thing. Like at the bench, I’ll be looking at a customer email, then I notice a saw mark, then there’s a noise outside, and suddenly I realize I’ve completely lost the thread of the work order.\n\nAnd with my kind of work, you can’t really do that. I mean, a tiny measurement or one note about a stone—if I lose track of that, it’s not just annoying, it can mess up the whole job. But no, I wouldn’t say it’s been the same all through adulthood. It feels more like something that’s gotten really bad now."
